A forward date was unsafe because both articles are referenced by `post_url` from already-published posts and a forward-dated target fails the entire build. The pilot directed shifting the tail forward by one day so the last article lands immediately before the SpaceX series and the SpaceX series moves back a day. Executed: 28 articles from A203 at 2026-07-08 through A292 at 2026-08-04 each shifted forward one day, leaving A202 in place at 07-08. The SpaceX series now runs 2026-07-25 through 2026-08-05. The `hdl_thread` series is now strictly monotonic by date at indices 1 through 5 mapping to 2026-03-13, 07-07, 07-08, 07-09, and 07-10. Renames were performed latest-first so no rename could land on an occupied filename, and 172 `post_url` references across 29 files were rewritten in the same change, since `post_url` resolves by filename and an unresolved tag fails the whole build. Verified: build clean with zero Liquid errors, 294 posts in and 293 HTML out with the single exclusion being A292, now dated 2026-08-05 and correctly withheld until that date arrives; zero unresolvable `post_url` targets among posts; zero filename-versus-front-matter mismatches. The four unresolvable targets that remain are all in `_drafts/` and are the documented, expected cross-referencing interlock. **Roughly 29 published URLs change as a result**, which the pilot authorized. **Not changed, awaiting pilot decision.** Five legacy dates still carry more than one article: 2016-01-08 with four, plus 2016-02-18, 2016-03-17, 2016-03-19, and 2017-01-25 with two each, twelve articles in total. Their timestamps are hours apart and look like authentic same-day publishing from the blog's early era rather than editorial placement, so moving them would rewrite real publication history and 404 twelve live URLs. |
| 2026-08-05 | Legacy date collisions resolved, completing the no-collision requirement. Seven articles from the 2016 and 2017 era moved onto subsequent free days, per pilot instruction. On each contested date the EARLIEST article by timestamp keeps the original date and later ones move forward to the next unoccupied day, which preserves the publication order recorded in the timestamps. 2016-01-08 carried four articles and now carries one: A2 at 02:49 stays, A1 at 03:07 moves to 01-09, A3 at 07:12 to 01-11, and A4 at 17:35 to 01-12, skipping 01-10 because A5 already occupies it. A17 moves from 2016-02-18 to 02-22, the next free day, since 02-19 through 02-21 are occupied; this places A17 after A19 through A21 in date order while its article number is lower, which is acceptable for the legacy block where numbers were assigned retroactively. A25 moves from 2016-03-17 to 03-18, A28 from 2016-03-19 to 03-20, and A50 from 2017-01-25 to 01-27. All seven had ZERO inbound `post_url` references, so no cross-links needed rewriting. Verified: build clean with no Liquid errors, 294 posts in and 293 HTML out with the single exclusion being the forward-dated 2026-08-05 article, zero unresolvable `post_url` targets among posts, zero filename-versus-front-matter mismatches, and **zero date collisions anywhere in the corpus**. Seven further published URLs change. **Caveat recorded for the future.** These timestamps look like authentic same-day publishing from the blog's earliest era rather than editorial placement, so the recorded dates for these seven articles no longer match the day they were actually written. The pilot directed the change with that tradeoff stated. |
| 2026-08-05 | Date-collision work corrected after a verification error, and timestamps normalized to UTC. **The error.** The previous collision checks keyed on the FRONT MATTER date, but the published URL is derived from the UTC instant, and 19 legacy posts carried `+0900` stamps whose UTC date falls a day earlier. Checking the wrong key reported zero collisions while two remained at URL level: 2016-02-06 carried A10 and A11, and 2016-02-21 carried A21 and A17. The second was introduced by the preceding legacy spread, which moved A17 onto 2016-02-22 believing it free; its `+0900 07:53` stamp resolves to 2016-02-21 in UTC, landing on A21. The timezone shift had also been masking the A10 and A11 pair for years. This is the same UTC-versus-local distinction that motivated pinning `timezone: UTC` earlier in the day; the insight was not carried into the collision check. **The fix.** All 19 non-UTC timestamps were converted to the identical instant expressed in UTC, for example `2016-02-22 07:53:00 +0900` becoming `2016-02-21 22:53:00 +0000`. The moment is unchanged and only its representation differs, so no published URL moves; this was verified by diffing a local build against the live sitemap, which showed the 19 normalizations and their 15 accompanying filename corrections producing ZERO URL changes. The corpus now uses a single offset throughout, so front matter date, filename date, UTC date, and URL date are one value rather than four that can disagree. The two genuine collisions were then spread by the same rule as before: A11 from 2016-02-06 to 02-09 and A17 from 2016-02-21 to 02-22, both being the later article by timestamp on their date. **Final state, verified against a clean build:** zero date collisions, zero filename-versus-front-matter mismatches, zero non-UTC offsets, zero unresolvable `post_url` targets among posts, and a URL diff against live showing exactly the 2 intended changes and nothing else. **Method rule earned.** When a system derives a value through a transformation, verify against the TRANSFORMED value, not the input. Checking front matter dates for collisions was checking the input to a timezone conversion whose output is what readers and the build actually use. |
| 2026-08-05 | Local build brought to near-parity with the deploy, which immediately exposed two live defects. **The bundle was never broken.** It had been recorded for some time that the local bundle fails on macOS from a gem-environment issue, and every verification this session therefore used a Gemfile-free scratch copy with `jekyll-archives` stripped. `bundle check` showed the real situation: the gems were simply not installed. `bundle install` succeeded on the first attempt, native extensions included, giving Jekyll 4.4.1 locally against the 4.2.2 system gem previously used, matching `Gemfile.lock` and therefore CI. **Parity measured, not assumed.** `JEKYLL_ENV=production bundle exec jekyll build --baseurl ""` produces 451 pages against the live sitemap's 450 URLs; the difference is two asset PDFs the sitemap lists and three pages carrying `sitemap: false`, namely `/404.html`, `/search/`, and `/stats/`. 152 category archives and the tag archive now build locally, where the stripped build produced none. Remaining gaps are `_downloads.rb`, requiring pandoc and the TeX toolchain, and the Pagefind index, requiring node; both degrade gracefully and show up in a crawl only as absent `.pdf`, `.epub`, and `/pagefind/` targets. **Two live defects found immediately.** A link crawl of the faithful build reported 4 genuine failures against the stripped build's 740 phantom ones. Two were the absent Pagefind assets. The other two were real: `/categories/c++/` and `/categories/no_std/` return 404 on the live site. `jekyll-archives` slugifies category names, generating `/categories/c/` and `/categories/no-std/`, while three templates emitted the raw category name verbatim. Five posts across two categories were affected. Fixed by applying Liquid's `slugify` in `_layouts/post.html`, `_includes/header.html`, and `categories.html`. The change was proven safe before application by evaluating `Jekyll::Utils.slugify` over every category in the corpus: exactly two differ from their raw form and both targets already exist, so the other 152 archives are untouched. A rebuild confirms zero genuinely broken internal links across all 451 pages. **Method conclusion.** The stripped build was not merely incomplete, it was actively misleading in both directions: it invented 740 broken links that were fine in production, and it concealed two that were genuinely broken. A verification environment that differs from production in an uncontrolled way produces false results in both directions, and the fix is to close the gap rather than to annotate it. |
| 2026-08-05 | Non-content improvements implemented per pilot direction. **Guards added.** `_verify.py` is a committed, offline, deterministic check of every corpus invariant repaired during this session, run in CI immediately after checkout so a defect fails in seconds rather than after the multi-minute toolchain install. It checks front matter presence and required keys, filename date against front matter date, UTC offsets, date collisions, duplicate article numbers, reference integrity covering undefined, unused and duplicate anchors, block-wise link-definition ordering, shadowed first categories that would 404 behind a sibling Pages site, math validity covering delimiter balance, environments nested inside display math, double subscripts and MathJax-only macros, prose style, and word-frequency outliers. Errors fail the build; warnings, including word frequency and drafts dated onto occupied days, report without failing. **The script found two bugs in itself before it found any in the corpus**, which is the point of testing a detector against known-good data: the double-subscript check scanned past a bare `^n` to an unrelated brace elsewhere in the file and reported seven false positives on ordinary summation limits, and the delimiter-balance check counted `$$` inside a Makefile in a highlight block. Both fixed, after which the corpus reported exactly two errors, both genuine prose style violations that had survived every previous pass: an en dash in a time range in the 2026-01-20 timezones article and em dashes around a clause in the 2026-01-31 Claude Code article. Repaired, and prose dashes across the corpus are now zero. Table cells retain en dashes in time ranges, which the check deliberately does not police. **Scheduled publication.** GitHub Pages builds on push, not on a timer, so under `future: false` a forward-dated post remained a 404 past its own date until an unrelated push happened, defeating the dated-series cadence. A `schedule:` trigger at 00:17 UTC now runs the workflow daily. Two caveats are recorded in the workflow itself: GitHub disables scheduled workflows after 60 days without repository activity, and cron firing is best effort. **Deploy cost.** The last run spent 7m31s installing the TeX toolchain and 13m22s generating downloads against 13s of actual site build. `_downloads.rb` is now content-addressed: each post's output is keyed on a SHA256 of its preprocessed body plus every option affecting output, cached under `.cache/downloads`, and copied into place on a hit, so only genuinely changed posts regenerate. A cache hit still places the file, because a post can move without its content changing. Verified end to end with a stub pandoc that writes a source-identifying payload: a cold run generated 293 PDFs and 293 EPUBs, a warm run served all 586 from cache, and the placed files hold 293 distinct contents across 293 posts, proving no cross-contamination. Apt archives are cached to a user-owned directory, since `actions/cache` runs as the runner and cannot read root-owned `/var/cache/apt/archives`. **Preview parity.** `_preview.sh` gained a `FUTURE` toggle. It still defaults to `--future`, preserving existing behaviour, but `FUTURE=0` matches the live site, and the script now prints which mode it is in. **Declined.** Redirects for the roughly 54 URLs changed today, and external link-rot monitoring, both at pilot direction; historical URLs are considered worth capturing and rot is not treated as a defect. Fabricated references remain a concern and are the subject of separate network-dependent citation checking rather than rot checking. |
| 2026-08-05 | Six follow-up improvements implemented per pilot direction. **A, citation verification.** `_verify_citations.py` pairs every DOI-bearing link definition with the reference-list text that cites it, resolves the DOI through Crossref, and compares author and title. Validated against the actual 2026-08-02 incident before use: it flags the fabricated pairing of "Space Traffic Management Priorities" against DOI `10.1111/jofi.12855` as a mismatch and passes the correct Kleibergen and Zhan pairing. Two of its own bugs were fixed first, a DOI regex truncating at a parenthesis and an inability to distinguish a nonexistent identifier from one registered outside Crossref, the latter resolved by querying the DOI registration agency API. **Findings across 292 checked citations: 259 correct, 29 MISMATCHES where the DOI resolves to a different work, 18 NONEXISTENT where the registration agency reports "DOI does not exist", 2 weak and 2 registered with DataCite and therefore unverifiable here.** Spot checks confirm the mismatches are real: "Starwisp: An Ultra-Light Interstellar Probe" resolves to "Development of the minimum friction adiabatic engine", and "Comparison of Hand-Drying Methods" resolves to "Preventing invasive candida infections". The worst-affected articles are 2026-03-05 von Neumann probes with 10 and 2026-03-06 error correction with 7. Remediation is content work and was not attempted. **B, pre-push hook.** `_hooks/pre-push` runs the checks in about three seconds against a roughly seventeen-minute CI rejection; enable with `git config core.hooksPath _hooks`, bypass with `--no-verify`. **C, social preview.** `_includes/head.html` now emits `og:image` and `twitter:image` from `page.image` or a site-level `og_image`, and upgrades the Twitter card to `summary_large_image` when one is present. Both tags are omitted entirely when unset, because a tag pointing at a missing or 48-pixel image previews worse than none. No image was invented; `_config.yml` documents the 1200x630 asset the pilot must supply. **D, Sass migration.** `sassc` 2.4.0 is LibSass-based and unmaintained; the project now uses `jekyll-sass-converter` 3.1.0 on `sass-embedded` 1.102.0. Proven safe rather than assumed: a semantic comparison of the generated CSS found 236 selectors on both sides with zero added or removed, and exactly two declaration differences, `grid-column: 1 / -1` against `1/-1` and equivalent `calc()` spacing. Colour output changes notation from hex to percentage `rgb()` but converts back exactly. Dart Sass emits deprecation warnings for `@import` and legacy colour functions, which are future work ahead of Dart Sass 3.0. **E, warning policy.** Warnings fell from 57 to 21 by fixing real defects and documenting genuine false positives rather than muting them. Contractions inside quotation marks are no longer flagged, since quoted titles and error strings are not the author's prose; that alone removed seven of eight, and the remaining four genuine contractions were corrected. MathJax-only macro warnings now fire only for macros `_downloads.rb` does NOT shim. `_verify_exemptions.yml` records per-article exemptions with a written reason each, porting the exclusion table from the throwaway word-pass script into a durable, auditable file. **F, draft checks.** Drafts now receive the full battery, reported under a `draft-` prefix as warnings so work in progress cannot fail the build while its defects stay visible before publication. The debug-tag check is skipped for drafts, which have no article number until publication assigns one. |
| 2026-08-05 | All 47 broken citations repaired. From 29 mismatches and 18 nonexistent identifiers to ZERO hard problems, verified against a cleared cache. **Method, designed so the repair could not reproduce the defect.** Candidates were proposed by Crossref bibliographic search scored on title-token overlap plus author-surname and year agreement, and NOTHING was applied automatically on that score alone. Each proposal was independently re-resolved through Crossref and re-assessed against the citation text before any file was written; 39 passed and were applied, 0 were refused. Several turned out to be single-digit transcription errors rather than fabrications, such as `10.1016/j.jhin.2014.11.006` for `...007` and `10.1016/0167-2789(84)90259-2` for `...90256-2`. **Eight needed individual handling.** Two had wrong TITLES as well as wrong identifiers, so fixing the DOI alone would have left a mislabelled reference: the Nicholson and Forgan paper is "Slingshot Dynamics for Self-Replicating Probes", not "Galactic Exploration by Directed Self-Replicating Probes", and Eigen 1993 is "Viral Quasispecies" in Scientific American, not "The Quasispecies Concept" in Annual Review of Biophysics. Both labels were corrected alongside the DOI. Six more were resolved by targeted search: the Best, Sandoe and Wilcox toilet-lid aerosolization paper, the Tabib-Azar MEMS logic-gate work, whose full author list confirmed Tabib-Azar despite the search ranking, and Turner's Total Functional Programming, whose correct identifier `10.3217/jucs-010-07-0751` is registered with DataCite rather than Crossref and therefore reports as unverifiable rather than wrong. **Two could not be confirmed as cited and were redirected to verified works rather than guessed**, following the 2026-08-02 precedent of removing rather than inventing. Both sit in Future Reading sections and support no argument. The Ellery citation claimed a 2020 Artificial Life article that no search could locate; it now points at Ellery's verified 2017 ECAL paper "Building Physical Self-Replicating Machines". The Hein and Baxter JBIS paper is absent from Crossref, so the citation now uses the arXiv preprint, whose identity was confirmed by reading the `citation_title` and `citation_author` metadata rather than by trusting a 200 response, exactly the trap this whole exercise exists to catch. **Residual, all false positives of the heuristic and none a defect:** two abbreviated titles where the author matches and the cited title is a shortened form of the real one, and four uses of the Turner DataCite identifier. |
| 2026-01-27 | A295-A296: Wire Formats, a two-article mini-series written to fill the only interior gap remaining in the 2026 calendar, the two-day slot between the WebAssembly-on-Jekyll post at 2026-01-26 and the constant-AMM-mathematics post at 2026-01-29. Shared main title "Wire Formats" with subtitles "What They Are" and "Implementation Tradeoffs". **A295** defines the class through three membership properties, namely that a representation is external, agreed in advance, and unambiguous over its domain, then argues that three families usually treated as separate disciplines all satisfy it: data interchange encodings carrying application values, protocol framing carrying conversation structure, and instruction encodings carrying programs. The argument that instruction encodings belong in the class is flagged in the Epistemic State section as an argument rather than a reported consensus. 203 lines, 5 display equations covering self-description overhead, varint length, framing efficiency, and mean instruction size. **A296** takes nine tradeoffs that cut across all three families, namely where the schema lives, how a reader finds the end of a unit, what happens on encountering the unrecognised, the price of inspectability, canonical encoding, compactness against decode cost, alignment and endianness, ossification, and how the choices interact. It closes by showing that several combinations are mutually contradictory, so a format cannot be evaluated against a checklist of desirable properties. 215 lines, 6 display equations. Both are analytical essays carrying the genre's Epistemic State and Out of Scope sections. **Citation discipline.** Every external URL was checked, and each of the seven cited RFCs was confirmed by fetching its title rather than by accepting a 200, following the rule established during the 2026-08-05 citation audit that a response code does not verify a citation. References are specifications and standards rather than secondary literature, which keeps the verifiable surface high. **Cross-linking, and a correction to the convention as applied.** The initial draft restricted itself to targets dated before 2026-01-27, treating back-reference-only as a chronological rule. The pilot corrected this: the convention exists to guarantee that a `post_url` target is present in the build, and every article dated 2026-08-04 or earlier is already in the build regardless of whether it precedes the referring article. Only the forward-dated 2026-08-05 article is unsafe. The correct rule is therefore that these articles may link any published article dated 2026-08-04 or earlier, and the conservative reading had excluded the most relevant material in the corpus. Six forward links were added on that basis, each carrying an argument rather than decorating: Keleusma's self-hosting strategy appears twice, in A295 because it treats a bytecode as a versioned artifact with a stated compatibility boundary, and in A296 because it records a bytecode-format change as a decision requiring authorisation, which is the ossification argument stated from the inside; the fixup-table and single-pass-validation articles support the claim that a decoder unable to look ahead faces the forward-reference problem in the same form compilers do; the bounded-working-memory article supports the case where the decoder rather than the link is the constrained resource; and the HTML hypermedia article supports the observation that machine-readable structure is the same axis the hypermedia literature already catalogues. The earlier back-references remain, with ION-DTN serving protocol framing, WebAssembly and Solana and ARM assembler serving instruction encodings, and the no_std Rust articles serving the embedded density argument. Fifteen distinct `post_url` targets across the pair, none future-dated, all resolving. Both dates had already passed, so batch publication resolved the internal cross-reference immediately and the build confirms zero unresolved `post_url` tags. **Verification.** `_verify.py` reports zero errors, prose style is clean with no dashes, contractions, prose parentheticals, or prose semicolons, the build completes with no Liquid errors, both articles render at their expected permalinks, and the series navigation generates correctly as Part 1 and Part 2 of 2. With these two published, 2026 carries 217 articles across 2026-01-01 through 2026-08-05 with **zero interior gaps and zero date collisions**. Next available article number A297. |
| 2026-01-27 | A295-A296 equation-density review. Display equations rose from 5 to 10 in A295 and from 6 to 12 in A296, with inline expressions at 31 and 43. **The genre convention constrains this pass and was followed rather than overridden.** `MATHJAX_CONVENTIONS.md` gives the analytical essay target as "as the argument requires, often zero" and states explicitly that math must not be padded to hit a target, in contrast to the subsystem deep-dive band of roughly 10 to 16. The rule applied here was therefore to add an equation only where the prose already made a quantitative claim that the equation states more precisely, and to add nothing that merely decorates. Every addition replaced or sharpened an existing verbal claim. **A295 additions.** The unambiguity membership property, previously stated only in words, is now the requirement that the decode relation be a function, which also sets up the contrast with canonicity in the companion article. The Protocol Buffers field tag is given as `\tau = 8f + w`, which explains why low field numbers are worth reserving. The varint bet is bounded by the crossover `x < 2^{49}`, below which a varint is strictly shorter than a fixed eight-byte encoding. Framing overhead is extended to the nested case, since encapsulation charges every layer. Compressed instruction density is given as a function of the compressed fraction. **A296 additions.** HPACK effectiveness is expressed as a hit-rate-weighted mean header size. The length-prefix resource-exhaustion hazard is stated as a bound the reader must enforce before allocating, with the observation that a check after allocation is not a check. Delimiter escaping is given an expected expansion factor. Schema evolution is stated as conditions on the composition of the writer's encode and the reader's decode under a version ordering, which makes backward and forward compatibility symmetric rather than informal. Canonicity is given as the cardinality condition on the encoding set, with the explicit note that it is strictly stronger than unambiguity, since unambiguity constrains bytes to values and canonicity constrains values to bytes. Version-negotiation testing burden is bounded between `k` and `k^2`. **Numerical claims were checked rather than asserted.** The varint crossover was confirmed at the boundary, with `2^{48}` taking seven varint bytes and `2^{49}` taking eight. The compressed-instruction claim that halving half the stream saves a quarter was confirmed. The HPACK worked figure of under six bytes was confirmed at 5.9. The claim that field numbers below sixteen give a single-byte tag was confirmed at the boundary, with field 15 yielding a one-byte tag and field 16 yielding two. Both articles verify clean, style is clean, and the build carries no Liquid errors. |
| 2026-01-27 | A295-A296 reference-density review, primary sources. Reference entries rose from 28 to 38 in A295 and from 19 to 23 in A296, with 33 specification and standards references, 6 books, 18 related posts, and 4 primary research papers across the pair. **Method.** The pass began by extracting every format named in prose and checking it against the reference list, which found seven formats discussed in A295 but never cited, namely MessagePack, FlatBuffers, the Extensible Markup Language, the Baudot code, the Network File System, the Transmission Control Protocol, and HTTP version 1.1. Each is now cited at the point of first substantive mention rather than appended to the list. Apache Thrift was added alongside the other reaction-against-text formats. **Four primary research papers were added, each attached to an argument the article already made.** `Sassaman, Patterson and Bratus 2013` on formal language theory supports the claim in A295 that a format permitting two readings of one byte sequence creates an attack surface, which the article previously asserted without support. `Haas and colleagues 2017` is the primary WebAssembly paper and now accompanies the specification reference. In A296, `Sassaman, Patterson and Bratus 2012`, titled "A Patch for Postel's Robustness Principle", lands directly on the permissive-reader section, where the article had described liberal acceptance without naming the principle or its modern revision; `RFC 9413` was added alongside it as the standards-track restatement. `Cohen 1981`, the paper that gave the field its endianness vocabulary, supports the observation that agreeing matters more than which convention is agreed. `RFC 8999` was added to the ossification section because publishing the invariant subset converts an implicit dependency surface into an explicit one, which is a sharper example than header encryption alone. **Verification.** All four DOIs were confirmed through Crossref by author and title before use, not by response code, which matters because `doi.org` returns 202 for the IEEE identifiers and 403 for the ACM one under bot detection. All 32 distinct URLs across the pair resolve. The citation checker reports zero hard problems. The definition-ordering guard in `_verify.py` caught the insertions as unsorted and the block-wise sorter corrected them, which is the first time the guards added earlier today caught a defect in new work rather than in the historical corpus. Build clean, zero unresolved `post_url` tags, zero literal anchors in the rendered output. |
| 2026-01-27 | A295-A296 publication review under the standing no-length-limit, no-reference-limit, comprehensive-survey directive. A295 grew from 233 to 329 lines and 38 to 65 references; A296 from 253 to 294 lines and 23 to 29 references. Equations held at 10 and 12, since the genre convention forbids padding and the equation pass had already taken every claim that warranted formalising. **A295 expansion.** The history gained the remote-procedure-call decade between ASN.1 and the web, which establishes that a wire format is rarely deployed alone but arrives attached to an interface definition language, a code generator, and a service model. A character-encoding passage was added, since UTF-8 is a variable-length integer encoding with a self-synchronising framing property and a canonical form, which makes it an instance of every theme in the article rather than the aside it is usually treated as. The interchange survey gained BSON and Amazon Ion as the keep-the-model-replace-the-bytes position, Simple Binary Encoding as the fixed-offset extreme used in electronic trading, and Borsh and Ethereum recursive length prefix encoding as the determinism-over-speed extreme, closing on the observation that most named formats are combinations of four binary choices and that absent combinations are usually contradictory rather than unexplored. The framing survey was extended across the whole stack from Ethernet and the Internet Protocol through UDP, TCP, SCTP, TLS records, WebSocket, HTTP/3, MQTT, AMQP, and gRPC, with the observation that a single gRPC call is framed at least four times and that TCP destroying message boundaries is the most consequential framing decision in the field's history. The instruction-encoding survey gained the x86 and ARM and Thumb spectrum, the managed-runtime bytecodes including the Java class file format, ECMA-335, Dalvik, and LLVM bitcode, and the Berkeley Packet Filter instruction set now standardised as RFC 9669, whose verify-before-execute posture matches WebAssembly's under a different threat model. **A296 expansion.** Two sections were added, on schema languages and what generates from them, covering the generated-code against reflective-decoding tradeoff and the point that a notation unable to express optionality cannot describe a message that adds a field, and on streaming against whole-message processing, including the observation that canonical sorted-key requirements defeat streaming encoders by forcing exactly the buffering streaming exists to avoid. The tradeoff count was corrected from nine to eleven in the prose and the Epistemic State. **The ossification section moved from assertion to measurement**, which is the most substantive change in the pair. It now cites Honda, Nishida and Raiciu 2011 on whether new TCP options can still traverse the public internet, Edeline and Donnet 2020 quantifying path brokenness against TCP options, the Papastergiou, Fairhurst and Ros 2017 survey of de-ossification work, and RFC 9170 on why an unexercised extension point will not work when needed. The Epistemic State now marks the generalisation from those TCP findings to wire formats as a class as this article's extrapolation rather than a claim the cited authors make. The unrecognised-handling section gained the language-theoretic security construction of Anantharaman, Millian and Bratus 2017. **Verification.** All 57 distinct URLs across the pair resolve, with 202 and 403 responses confined to IEEE and ACM bot detection on identifiers already confirmed through Crossref by author and title. The citation checker reports zero hard problems. `_verify.py` reports zero errors. One genuine style violation introduced during expansion, a prose semicolon, was caught by the sweep and removed. Build clean, zero unresolved `post_url` tags, zero literal anchors, rendered word counts 5202 and 4612. |
| 2026-08-05 | X-Planes series planning and a new genre document, no articles drafted. **Series shape.** 72 articles, A297 through A368, editorially dated 2025-10-06 through 2025-12-16, one per day unbroken, ending flush against the 2025-12-17 Solana article that begins the run to 2026-08-05. Verified as zero collisions. Back-dated rather than forward-dated, so the series publishes immediately on push and does not depend on the still-unproven daily cron. Parity target is the History of SpaceX medians of 1345 lines, 72 display equations, and 306 reference definitions, with depth allowed to fall where the record does not support it. **Roster verification, and a correction the pilot caught.** The roster was checked against `designation-systems.net`, last updated 3 January 2026, which lists the X-series anomalies and states that the next sequential number is X-69. That statement was initially read as an upper bound, and X-76 was therefore excluded. The pilot challenged the assumption that designations are assigned monotonically and supplied the DARPA release, which confirms the Bell Textron X-76 SPRINT and states that the number was chosen as a deliberate nod to 1776 for the country's 250th anniversary. Assignment is therefore not sequential, the reference describes only the next unused slot, and X-76 was restored. Wikipedia's own navigation template independently labels X-76 as non-sequential. **Nine anomaly cases were identified, seven of them only after abandoning the sequential assumption.** X-23 is generally attributed to the Martin Marietta SV-5D PRIME while USAF nomenclature records reportedly show X-23A was never assigned. X-39 was reserved on 23 April 1997 for the AFRL FATE program but no written allocation request followed. X-41 is a still-classified vehicle in the DARPA FALCON program with no released specifications. X-42 is documented inconsistently, described by one source as an expendable liquid-propellant upper stage and by another as a military spaceplane test vehicle, with no dedicated article anywhere. X-44 carries two different aircraft, the Lockheed Martin MANTA and a separate UAV. X-52 was requested in 2006 and refused over possible confusion with the B-52, and the program became the X-53. X-58 and X-67 were skipped when their slots went to the Kratos XQ-58 Valkyrie and the General Atomics XQ-67A, which is the parallel unmanned series consuming numbers from the same pool. X-69 through X-75 remain unassigned and were leapfrogged. **A title correction was made and one proposed correction was withdrawn.** A338 changed from "X-41 Maneuvering Reentry Vehicle" to "X-41 Common Aero Vehicle", because the former was a contested description rather than the designation's name. A proposed rename of A339 to "X-42, an Upper Stage with an Aircraft Number" was withdrawn as overreach, since it embedded a conclusion drawn from a disagreement between two sources of unequal weight into a title, where the Epistemic State is the correct home for that uncertainty. **New genre.** Neither existing genre fits a research aircraft, so `_docs/writing/RESEARCH_AIRCRAFT_STRUCTURE.md` defines a fourth. A research aircraft exists to answer one question, and that question behaves as an architectural keystone, so systems are dimensioned against it as in a subsystem deep-dive, while programme origin, flight-test record, and the Epistemic State come from the analytical essay. The document defines a twelve-section order and three article classes with explicit line, equation, and reference bands, namely full aircraft, documentation-poor, and designation anomaly, on the principle that depth is a function of the surviving record rather than of effort. It states that the Epistemic State never folds regardless of length, because on a short article it carries the statement of what the record does not settle. `ARTICLE_GENRES.md`, `POST_STRUCTURE.md`, `STYLE_GUIDE.md`, and the writing README were updated from three genres to four, with all stale counts swept and all documentation links verified to resolve. **Settled before drafting.** Series slug `x_planes`, series title `X-Planes`, articles written from current knowledge with the anachronism noted where information postdates the editorial date, and overlap with the History of Rocketplanes article accepted for the eight designations it already covers. |
| 2026-08-08 | A311: X-Planes Bell X-14 researched, written, verified, and committed. Draft pass only; not pushed and not published. **The article is the fifteenth in the X-Planes series and the first whose subject is an instrument rather than a vehicle.** The X-14 is the variable-stability aircraft that produced the attitude control-power criteria A310 had to borrow anachronistically and flag in its own epistemic state, so the article opens by naming the debt. The keystone is the criteria themselves, and the framing carried throughout is that a research aircraft built as an instrument has no mission to fail at, which shifts weight from the flight record onto What the Data Changed. **Keystone documents and an archive defect.** NASA TN D-1328, Rolls and Drinkwater 1962, is the origin of the criteria; the NTRS search endpoint returns it for no phrasing of its own title and the record carries no downloadable document, so it was located through its citation in a later report and the article takes its quantitative claims from the complete successor TN D-2701 instead. The Source Base states this. No pilot ratings are asserted anywhere, because the figure carrying them did not survive text extraction, and inventing them would have manufactured the headline number. **Central sizing result.** The reaction-control bleed fraction is inverted rather than estimated: the tip-turbine fan report states that halving the bleed returned four percent more engine thrust, which gives beta = 0.08/1.08 = 7.41 percent exactly. At the X-14A's reported 3,700 pound test weight and 1.1 to 1.2 available thrust-to-weight ratio, that bleed cost 397 pounds of thrust and consumed 34.9 to 51.8 percent of the aircraft's entire hover margin, so between a third and a half of everything it could lift beyond its own weight was spent on being controllable. **Results the sources do not state.** The roll inertia is recovered at 3,333 kg m2 from the maximum tested control power in one report and the replacement fan design thrust in another, giving a radius of gyration of 13.7 percent of span, mid-band for a fuselage-heavy layout, which validates the recovery; the fans were specified at exactly the existing maximum authority rather than above it, indicating bleed and not control power was the binding constraint. Control power at fixed bleed fraction falls inversely with span, so the required bleed grows linearly with size and a jet-lift aircraft with wingtip reaction controls exhausts a twenty percent thrust margin near 28 metres of span; a Do 31 sized vehicle would need 3,542 pounds of thrust at each wingtip. The original Viper-engined aircraft had a thrust-to-weight ratio of 0.843 to 0.946 at the weight the X-14A later hovered at, below unity on either reading of the contested engine rating, so the J85 re-engining was a precondition for the research programme rather than a performance upgrade. Control power shows sharply diminishing returns with an exponent of minus 0.26, so two and a half times the authority buys only 21 percent less time to correct a position error. The variable-stability system's synthesised damping and the pilot's control authority draw on one shared budget, which explains the source's otherwise modest phrase that the tested grid covered conditions to the ability of the X-14A. And gravity was not adjustable, so the aircraft reproduced lunar attitude dynamics exactly and lunar translation 2.46 times too fast, which is why the Lunar Landing Research Vehicle had to exist. **Two defects in the numerical spine, both caught by running it rather than reading it.** The repositioning analysis used a = g theta and searched tilt over a bounded range whose upper bound it then reported as the optimum for every control power above 1.4, so the printed optimum was the edge of the search interval rather than a minimum; rewritten with a = g tan theta, a thirty degree cap, and a hover-scale correction distance. Separately, the threshold section had assigned plausible-looking Cooper ratings to the three sampled control powers and interpolated a satisfactory boundary from them, and those ratings are in no source; removed and replaced with the resolution argument, which needs no ratings and shows the three-point grid locates a threshold only to within thirty to seventy-five percent of its own value. **Three defects caught by reading and counting, all of which passed every automated check.** The phrase "in its own right" ended one paragraph and opened the next after a later insertion landed behind a sentence ending the same way; the disturbance fraction and the radius-of-gyration fraction were both written as kappa, a symbol collision within one article, since resolved to sigma; and a sentence promised four subsections where five stood. **Method.** The master reference table was built from the A310 and A311 harvests together, which is a deliberate exception to the A310 single-directory rule rather than an inherited default, because the X-13 and X-14 genuinely share the jet-lift handling-qualities literature and the A310 pool already held the Drinkwater and Rolls work by name. Combined master 2,419 entries. | 2026-08-08 | A311: Equation-density review, the second of four passes. Committed, not pushed. Display equations rose from 51 to 103 across 22 edits, inside the 90 to 130 band, with nothing trimmed. Final state 1088 lines, 103 display equations, 95 references, 15,001 body words. **The pass corrected a claim inherited from A310.** The draft said position is the third integral of the pilot's control input, following the previous article, which had reasoned that position is the double integral of attitude and attitude is the integral of whatever the control does. Writing the transfer function down rather than the chain of integrals shows the second clause fails: a reaction nozzle produces a moment, a moment produces angular acceleration, so attitude is the double integral of control and position is the fourth integral, giving x(s)/u(s) = g CP / s^4 in the undamped case. The reconciliation is that the earlier description is the low-frequency case, since the plant with the rate term restored is g CP / (s^3 (s + D/I)), third order below the damping break frequency and fourth order above it. Hovering is therefore a third-order problem or a fourth-order one depending on how much damping the aircraft has, and the X-14A is the only aircraft in this series that could have demonstrated the distinction because it is the only one whose damping was an adjustable parameter. The distinction is not cosmetic: a fourth-order plant demands two derivatives of lead from the pilot rather than one, which is the clearest available explanation of the 1972 Ames simulator finding that attitude stabilisation gives the best handling qualities for the least control power, since attitude stabilisation removes two orders from the plant rather than merely assisting the pilot. The correction is recorded in the Epistemic State as a correction to the previous article. A310 was not edited, because its phrasing is the low-frequency case and both articles describe the same physics. **Four further results.** An optimally flown hover correction spends exactly half its time changing attitude and half translating, whatever the control power and whatever the distance, which falls out of the small-angle stationarity condition theta* = (1/2) sqrt(CP d / g) and was verified to give a term ratio of exactly one. The wind is confirmed as a position problem rather than an attitude problem on this aircraft as it was on the X-13, since a ten metre per second wind produces only 5.9 percent of the maximum control power as a moment but requires a permanent 1.36 degree tilt and costs 11.7 metres of drift in ten seconds uncorrected, which establishes that the disturbance sizing the control power is the gust and the manoeuvre rather than the steady wind. The overhead is now stated as a product: control consumes 34.9 to 51.8 percent of the hover margin and the disturbance allowance consumes two fifths of what that bought, leaving only 21 to 31 percent of the margin as manoeuvring authority in the pilot's hands. The pitch nozzle must be about forty percent stronger than the roll nozzle for the same control power because the moment arms differ and the aircraft is wider than it is long, and the J85-GE-19 installation bought about 590 pounds more lift than the J85-GE-5, which is what set how much authority the later experiments could afford to give away rather than the digital computer. **Two defects in the new spine, both caught by running it rather than reading it.** The closed form for the optimum tilt was written as (CP d / 4g)^(1/3) and missed the numeric optimum by six degrees; the correct form is (1/2) sqrt(CP d / g), and printing the numeric and the closed form side by side is what exposed it, since reporting the numeric alone would have left the article right by accident while the general relation it stated was wrong. Separately the diverter section printed a hardcoded constant where it claimed to sweep the deflection angle, so its entire table was meaningless; replaced with the actual component relations T_v = eta T cos delta and T_h = eta T sin delta. This is the seventh consecutive article in which computing before writing caught a wrong claim and the second in which it changed an argument rather than a number. **Verification.** All 31 newly introduced numbers re-derived independently and reproducing, on top of the 45 verified during the draft pass. `_verify.py` at the 0-error 21-warning corpus baseline from the repository root. | 2026-08-08 | A311: Publication review, the fourth and final pass. Committed and pushed. Not published. Final state 1515 lines, 109 display equations, 386 references, 19,934 body words. Contemporary references rose from 10 to 129, or 35.5 percent of dated, inside the 101 to 189 absolute range the series has held since A301, closing what had been the largest contemporary gap any article carried into a publication review. References finish 6 above the 380 ceiling, reported rather than trimmed under the standing no-reference-limit directive as A309 did, and nothing was trimmed at any point in any of the four passes, making A311 the sixth consecutive article to finish that way. **The pass computed before it surveyed and the computation produced the strongest result in the article.** Deriving control power for a multirotor from first principles, where attitude moments come from differential rotor thrust rather than compressor bleed, gives CP = c_g min(r-1,1) g / (2 kappa^2 b), which is inversely proportional to span. That is the same law and the same exponent the article derived for bleed-fed reaction nozzles, by a mechanism that shares no hardware with it, so the inverse-span dependence is not a property of reaction controls at all but of making moments with forces at the extremities of a vehicle whose thrust scales with its weight, and it survives complete replacement of the propulsion system. **Three further contemporary findings.** The cost of attitude control changed character rather than magnitude: the X-14A's bleed was a standing tax of 7.41 percent supplied whether or not the pilot commanded anything, whereas a multirotor's differential is zero-sum so the mean cost is zero and what the vehicle pays instead is headroom it needs anyway, meaning the constraint that dominated the X-14 has been dissolved rather than solved, which is the same verdict A310 reached about the X-13 by a different route. The constraint nonetheless moved rather than vanishing, since the multirotor relation falls to the X-14A's maximum at 17.3 metres of span at an air-taxi thrust margin, against the 28 metres at which this article found jet lift exhausting its bleed budget, so large hovering aircraft remain hard for a reason the X-14A measured. And the loop-order result from the equation pass gained a retrospective sting, because a modern attitude loop closes near 10 to 30 radians per second against the X-14A's synthesised damping breaks of 0.45 and 0.59 per second, so the aircraft sat in its fourth-order regime across the whole of the band its pilots actually worked in, which is the sharpest available explanation of why it was hard to fly and could only be stated once the modern comparison was computed. Representative vehicle control powers were 173, 54.2, and 4.33 radians per second squared for a small quadrotor, a cargo multirotor, and an air taxi, against the X-14A's 2.0. Thirteen subsections replaced eight, covering the modern derivation, the change in the character of the cost, the constraint's relocation, the authority budget as a moment polytope, control allocation as the modern handover, the loop order designed out by attitude command, the criterion moving to bandwidth and then to certification, handling qualities for vehicles with no pilot aboard, the shared division of authority, in-flight simulation and subscale testing, reduced gravity and the Ingenuity identification, ground effect as a vertiport problem, and the tip-fan failure mode surviving in electric drive. **Two defects found and fixed.** The URL-stability guard fired when the master was rebuilt on the 687 new records and caught two cited anchors that had drifted to different documents, a biplane tail-sitter paper and a shared-control paper, both repointed to what the prose meant; this is the third consecutive article in which that guard has caught real drift and it would have been silent corruption without it. | 2026-08-08 | A312: X-Planes North American X-15 researched, written, verified, and committed. Draft pass only; not pushed and not published. **The keystone was chosen rather than discovered, which no previous article in the series has had to do.** The X-15's record is large enough that four candidate keystones are all well supported, so the article names aerodynamic heating, hypersonic stability and control, flight outside the atmosphere, and structures at temperature, rejects all four on the ground that they are consequences of a single quantity, and defends the choice of energy disposal. At its record speed the X-15's kinetic energy was 2.041 MJ/kg against roughly 0.904 MJ/kg to take a kilogramme of its own structure to melting and melt it, so the aircraft carried 2.26 times the energy needed to melt itself and 7.4 times the energy needed to reach the 1,200 F limit of its strength; that is what makes deceleration a thermal event rather than a nuisance and is the difference in kind between hypersonic flight and merely fast flight. **Central result.** A Sutton and Graves stagnation-point correlation at the record condition gives 64.8 W/cm2 while a structure at its design temperature radiating at emissivity 0.8 can reject 3.28, so the record flight asked the structure to reject 19.8 times its design capacity, which is why the X-15A-2 was covered in ablative coating, why that flight was the fastest ever made, and why nothing like it was attempted again; the correlation is validated against the reported 2,700 F leading-edge temperature, overshooting by 12.7 percent, and the conclusion survives doubling the assumed nose radius. **Results the sources do not state.** The speed and altitude records are one energy budget spent two ways at 2.347 and 1.964 MJ/kg, differing by 19.5 percent because Flight 188 was flown by the X-15A-2 with external tanks rather than because it was flown better. Converting the speed record's kinetic energy entirely into height gives 239.3 km against an actual altitude record of 108.0, so a little over half the available energy never reached apogee. The speed record was 89.9 percent of the ideal rocket delta-v. The heating rate falls with thinner air while the heat load rises, since the rate goes as sqrt(rho) V^3 and the time to shed a fixed energy goes as 1/rho, so rate and load are optimised by opposite trajectories and the X-15A-2 moved from a rate-limited to a load-limited thermal regime when it was coated. A 300 kelvin gradient alone yields the structure at 830 MPa, which is why the skin is corrugated and slotted for no load reason, and a 1.5 mm Inconel skin equilibrates in 7.5 seconds, which is what makes a hot structure rate-limited. And 99.8 percent of the energy the vehicle holds at its fastest must be disposed of before it lands. **The series thread arrives from the opposite direction**, since the X-13 and X-14 lost aerodynamic control authority because the vehicle was not moving while the X-15 loses it because there is no air, with a dynamic pressure ratio across a single flight exceeding 4,600. **An order-of-magnitude error was caught by verifying rather than by reading.** The aerodynamic-to-reaction crossover was written as 6,280 Pa and computes to 628, moving the handover band from between 30 and 50 km to about 55 km; this is the eighth consecutive article in which computing before writing caught a wrong claim and the first where the error was a full order of magnitude. The corrected value produced an unplanned result, namely that Flight 91's burnout at 53.6 km sits within 1.4 km of the crossover, so on a high flight the engine stops at roughly the altitude where the aerodynamic surfaces stop working and the pilot loses thrust and aerodynamic control at nearly the same moment. **Two omissions were found by surveying the reference pool rather than by reading the draft**, the MH-96 adaptive flight control system, which was fitted to the airframe subsequently lost, and shock interference heating, which is what nearly destroyed the speed-record flight and which the draft described without citing; both were added. Two documents cited in the draft were absent from the harvest and were added to the NTRS detail fetch by identifier rather than by repointing the prose at a different document, which is the A311 archive-retrieval lesson applied. **Method.** The master was built from the A312 harvest alone, since the X-15 shares nothing with the jet-lift handling-qualities literature of A310 and A311; combined master 673 entries. **Verification.** 77 reference definitions, 62 external URLs, zero duplicates, zero orphans. | 2026-08-08 | A312: Equation-density review, the second of four passes. Committed, not pushed. Display equations rose from 48 to 90 across 22 edits, landing exactly on the floor of the 90 to 130 band, with nothing trimmed. Final state 929 lines, 90 display equations, 77 references, 10,729 body words. **The pass answered the question the keystone posed and the draft left open**, namely how much of the energy the vehicle sheds ends up inside its own structure rather than in the air behind it. The route is the Reynolds analogy: the heat entering the wall divided by the friction work done at it collapses to c_p (T_aw - T_w) / V^2, in which the velocity cancels out of the numerator entirely, so the fraction depends only on how far the wall sits below the adiabatic wall temperature measured against the vehicle's kinetic energy. At the record condition that is 43.0 percent for a cold wall, 27.7 percent at the design limit, and 7.2 percent at the temperature Knight's leading edges actually reached. A hot wall therefore absorbs a smaller fraction than a cold one, which means running hot is part of the mechanism by which a hot structure protects itself rather than merely something it tolerates; the framing did not anticipate that. **A wrong claim was caught by computing further inside the same pass.** The energy fraction reaching the structure was first written on an assumed friction fraction of 35 percent of total drag, which gave about ten percent to the structure and a heat load exceeding the structure's absorptive capacity at a ratio of 1.19. Estimating the friction drag directly, from a turbulent flat-plate skin friction coefficient at the record Reynolds number of 3.2e7 over a plausible wetted area of 113 square metres, gives 8 to 26 percent depending on the drag coefficient and wetted area assumed and about 15 percent centrally, not 35. The corrected figure is four percent to the structure and a ratio of 0.51, so the conclusion inverts: the total heat load is comfortably within what the structure can hold, with roughly a factor of two in hand, and the binding constraint is the local rate rather than the global total. The corrected version is also the one consistent with the rest of the article, since a rate-limited hot structure should have load margin in hand and the erroneous version contradicted the article's own earlier finding; the two halves now agree and they did not have to. This is the ninth consecutive article in which computing before writing caught a wrong claim and the second in this article, after the crossover order-of-magnitude error found during the draft pass. Both the superseded figure and the correction are recorded explicitly in the Epistemic State. **The relation that closes the keystone was added.** Deceleration by drag at constant altitude integrates to a time to shed the energy, and evaluating it from the record speed to a landing speed gives 102 minutes at the record altitude, 40 at 25 km, 18 at 20 km, 8 at 15 km, and 4 at 10 km, against a total flight duration of 8 to 12 minutes. The aircraft therefore cannot dispose of its energy where it acquires it and must descend into denser air to do so, and descending into denser air is precisely what raises the heating rate since the rate goes as the square root of density. That is the keystone stated as a single trap and every other result in the sizing section is a term in it. **Six further results.** The stagnation temperature at the record is 2,271 K, so the air the aircraft is flying into is 2.46 times hotter than the metal is permitted to become, and 89.9 percent of the oncoming stream's total enthalpy is kinetic, so the heat is the aircraft's own energy arriving back at it. Newtonian impact theory gives Cp = 2 sin^2(theta), independent of Mach number, which is the whole advantage of the thick wedge fin and puts it a factor of 11 above a three-degree surface. The same relation puts the trim angle of attack at the record condition near 13.8 degrees, so a hypersonic aeroplane does not fly nose-first. The blunt-body trade eliminates the nose radius to give D_nose proportional to q_dot^-4, so halving the heating costs sixteen times the nose drag, which is why a re-entry capsule is blunt and an aeroplane that must glide several hundred kilometres home is not. The B-52 supplies under seven percent of the energy budget, so the aircraft makes the other ninety-three percent itself. And a 1.5 mm Inconel skin is thermally through-soaked in half a second and has no interior, which is why the design can be reasoned about as a surface in radiative balance with no thermal-lag margin to hide behind. **Verification.** All 43 newly introduced numbers re-derived independently and reproducing, on top of the 40 verified during the draft pass. `_verify.py` at the 0-error 21-warning corpus baseline from the repository root. Final state 1142 lines, 90 display equations, 252 references, 12,993 body words, with equations and references inside band, lines still approaching from below at 1142 against a 1300 floor, and nothing trimmed at any point. Primary sources are 220 of 233 dated, or 94.4 percent, second only to A311's 95.9 at the same stage. **The pool was too small and was harvested before it was audited**, per the rule earned in A310. The first sweep had returned 199 NTRS records and only 17 X-15-specific documents, which is not what a nine-year programme that reported to its sponsors in conference volumes actually produced, so rather than audit a thin pool and discover afterwards that it was thin, a supplementary sweep aimed at the threads the draft and equation passes opened added 82 NTRS, 100 DTIC, 122 period, and 39 OSTI records and took the master from 673 to 991. **Two documents were found that bear directly on derivations the equation pass produced rather than on anything the draft said.** Keener and Polek 1972 reports measurements of the Reynolds analogy for a hypersonic turbulent boundary layer on a nonadiabatic flat plate, which is precisely the relation the energy-partition result runs on and precisely the wall condition it runs at; the article had derived it from first principles without knowing it had been measured, and the measurement is now cited beside the derivation, with Thomas and Chung 1973 doing the same for the recovery factor. Edney 1968 is the canonical treatment of anomalous heat transfer and pressure distributions on blunt bodies in the presence of an impinging shock, published the year after the flight that demonstrated the effect on a crewed aircraft, and a cluster now sits around it comprising Creel 1974 on projections and cavities at Mach 8, Johnson and Kaufman 1974 on interference heating from shocks striking turbulent boundary layers, Kessler et al 1971, and Birch and Rudy 1975, so the article can now say that a literature grew around the failure mode the X-15A-2 discovered by suffering it. **A second finding came from the facility literature.** Inventorying the hypersonic tunnel estate of the period establishes something the article could previously only assert, namely that a tunnel matches Mach number, or Reynolds number, or enthalpy, and not all three at once, which is the quantitative reason an aircraft was needed at all and why the discrepancies the X-15 revealed were worth the programme's cost. The same flight-versus-tunnel comparison was run again on the X-24B a decade later in Richardson 1976, so the calibration problem the X-15 existed to address had not been solved by its successors, and the hypersonic research airplane concepts of the 1970s were studied at length in the Penland series and none of them was built, which is why the X-15 remains the only crewed vehicle to have flown the regime. **Selection discipline mattered more than usual.** A loose theme filter surfaced uranium and niobium metallurgy, a Marine Corps acquisition management study, aerial cannon shells, titanium brazing, industrial wind tunnels, and ice crystals in hypersonic flow, all matching on keywords and none belonging in the article; every one was rejected by title inspection before anything was written, which is exactly what the dump-titles-before-writing rule exists for. Six link-text mismatches were caught by the invariant and repointed to the master displays, mostly author-capitalisation quirks in the archive metadata such as Orlik-rueckemann and Mctigue. The URL-stability guard fired no drift when the master was rebuilt over 318 new records. **Verification.** 252 reference definitions, zero duplicates, zero orphans. `_verify.py` at the 0-error 21-warning corpus baseline from the repository root. Zero contractions, em-dashes, en-dashes, prose colons, prose semicolons, prose parentheticals, doubled words, duplicate headings, unbalanced emphasis markers, lone dollar-delimited lines, or adjacent display-math seams. Genre section order correct. Isolated build succeeding with 90 rendered display blocks matching the source count exactly, Part 16 navigation, no unresolved reference links and no surviving Liquid tags. Citation density 19.32 per thousand body words, comparable to A311's final 20.2, with the densest section being Comparison With Ground Prediction at 47.7, which is a facility inventory bracketed by an argument rather than a bibliography dump. **Contemporary references stand at 13 of 233 dated, or 5.6 percent.** The absolute count is unchanged since the draft and the percentage fell only because this pass grew the denominator by 175. Against the 101 to 189 absolute count the series has held since A301, this is the largest contemporary gap any article has carried into a publication review, larger than A311's and A309's, and closing it is the next pass's principal task; the hypersonic literature is large and current, so the material exists. Next available article number A313. |
